I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the same way. I guess this feels more… stuck than stuff before.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped enjoying things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I mean, everybody has bad stretches, right? Like when I was younger I’d get really stressed with school or money, and I remember one semester where I basically lived on vending machine coffee and those little powdered donuts from the gas station—terrible idea, by the way, they leave that weird film in your mouth.\n\nBut depressed most days for a while like this? I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the same way. Yeah… it’s changed, but not in one clean direction, if that makes sense. Some weeks I barely touch the staff meal, even if it’s something I usually like. I’ll just pick at it and then get busy with tables and forget it’s sitting there.

I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the same way. I don’t feel that pull to answer like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? It’s kind of embarrassing, because it’s not even meals exactly, it’s more like I can’t tell what I need.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf the mortgage situation were suddenly resolved, do you think you would still feel depressed?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, like I’m going through the motions carrying plates and smiling because I’m supposed to, not because I feel anything good.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the mortgage thing definitely makes everything louder, like when a customer is tapping their card on the little machine before I’ve even put the bill in, and you’re already behind on three tables—sorry, that’s not really the question.

Even if that got fixed, I think I’d still have that heavy, empty feeling most days. Like I’m carrying plates around and smiling because I’m supposed to, not because I actually feel okay. The money stress doesn’t help, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only that.

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I keep feeling like I’ve really let my family down. We took on this mortgage, and I keep thinking it was my fault, like I pushed us into it or I should’ve known better. And then at work, if I mess up even something small—like an order or forgetting extra napkins—it feels like proof that I’m just useless.\n\nSorry, I know that sounds dramatic. I used to be pretty good at my job, you know, I could remember regulars’ drinks and all that. Now every little mistake just sticks in my head, and I keep thinking I deserve whatever happens because I caused all this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. It’s like this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. I’ll be at work carrying plates and smiling at people because that’s what you do, you know, the whole “how’s everything tasting?” thing, and I can hear myself saying it but I don’t really feel… anything good behind it. I’m just sort of going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after the mortgage situation happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It kind of started after we took on the condo mortgage. Not, like, the next morning exactly, but that’s when it began to settle in and just… not really leave.\n\nIt’s been about a year and eight months now, which sounds weirdly specific, but I remember because it lines up with the mortgage stuff. I keep thinking about all the paperwork and how I should’ve read things more carefully, and then I start thinking about other times I messed up money things, like even stupid stuff from years ago… but yeah, around then is when the depressed feeling really started.\n\nDoctor: Before this started around the mortgage, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed most days for a while?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I mean, everybody has bad stretches, right? Like when I was younger I’d get really stressed with school or money, and I remember one semester where I basically lived on vending machine coffee and those little powdered donuts from the gas station—terrible idea, by the way, they leave that weird film in your mouth.\n\nBut depressed most days for a while like this? I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the same way. I guess this feels more… stuck than stuff before.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped enjoying things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. Like, I used to enjoy messing around with the regulars at the restaurant, joking with them, remembering their little orders and making it fun. Now I mostly just keep my face straight and say what I need to say.\n\nAnd after shifts I used to text my brother, just stupid stuff sometimes, like something weird a customer said or a picture of the bus stop sign that’s been bent forever—sorry, that’s not really the point. But now I’ll see his message and just let it sit there. I don’t feel that pull to answer like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I worry about the mortgage stuff a lot, obviously, and then sometimes it branches off into, like, whether I’m doing enough at work or if I forgot something small, like leaving the coffee pot on—though I don’t even drink much coffee at home, so that’s kind of dumb.\n\nBut “many different things,” I’m not sure. It feels more like one thought grabs another thought, if that makes sense, and then I’m just kind of stuck following it around.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Um… sleep? I don’t know, honestly. I mean, I go to bed, or I’m supposed to go to bed, and then I’ll get distracted by something stupid like whether the sheets need washing or if the neighbors are dragging bins around outside.\n\nI haven’t really kept track of it. Some nights feel worse than others, I guess, but I couldn’t tell you in a clean way. Like, it’s not like I have a chart or anything.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage situation, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most days and lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: Before the mortgage thing? I… I’m not sure. I don’t really have it sorted in my head like that, like “this was two weeks” or “that was before.” Time gets kind of mushy when I think back on it.\n\nI mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure, but whether it was most days and losing interest at the same time… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it more. I remember stuff around work more than dates, like who was on the schedule or whether the soda machine was acting up, but that doesn’t really answer you.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t sit there counting the days exactly, but most days there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m at work carrying plates around and smiling because that’s what you do, you know, people don’t want their waiter looking miserable while they’re ordering soup or whatever… but it’s not because I actually feel good.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I mean… not like I’m planning something. I don’t have, you know, a plan to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut yeah, sometimes when I’m lying there awake and I know I have to be up early for a shift, I’ll think… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could skip the whole thing and not keep messing everything up. Then I start thinking about tables I forgot to refill water for, which is stupid, but it all kind of piles up.\n\nDoctor: Has your appetite changed much since this started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, but not in one clean direction, if that makes sense. Some weeks I barely touch the staff meal, even if it’s something I usually like. I’ll just pick at it and then get busy with tables and forget it’s sitting there.\n\nThen other nights I get home and I’m suddenly eating whatever’s in the kitchen, like crackers, leftovers, cereal, just… standing there, trying to feel settled or something. It’s kind of embarrassing, because it’s not even meals exactly, it’s more like I can’t tell what I need.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage situation were suddenly resolved, do you think you would still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the mortgage thing definitely makes everything louder, like when a customer is tapping their card on the little machine before I’ve even put the bill in, and you’re already behind on three tables—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nEven if that got fixed, I think I’d still have that heavy, empty feeling most days. Like I’m carrying plates around and smiling because I’m supposed to, not because I actually feel okay. The money stress doesn’t help, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll just pick at it and then get busy with tables and forget it’s sitting there.\n\nThen other nights I get home and I’m suddenly eating whatever’s in the kitchen, like crackers, leftovers, cereal, just… standing there, trying to feel settled or something. It’s kind of embarrassing, because it’s not even meals exactly, it’s more like I can’t tell what I need.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage situation were suddenly resolved, do you think you would still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the mortgage thing definitely makes everything louder, like when a customer is tapping their card on the little machine before I’ve even put the bill in, and you’re already behind on three tables—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nEven if that got fixed, I think I’d still have that heavy, empty feeling most days. Like I’m carrying plates around and smiling because I’m supposed to, not because I actually feel okay. The money stress doesn’t help, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I don’t know… I mean, I’m tired a lot, but it’s hard to tell if it’s “unusual” tired or just, you know, restaurant tired. Being on your feet for a whole shift, carrying trays, people changing their orders after you already put them in—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that one I notice at work. Like with the POS, I’ll be putting in table numbers or splitting checks, and I just don’t trust that I entered it right, even when it’s stuff I’ve done for years.

And inventory sheets too—numbers that should be simple, cases of soda or whatever, I’ll stare at the screen like… wait, did I just type that in? Is that the right line? Then someone asks me about ranch or a side of fries and I lose the whole thread.

So yeah, concentrating has been harder. Not all the time, but enough that it makes me second-guess myself.

I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the same way. I guess this feels more… stuck than stuff before.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped enjoying things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. Like, I used to enjoy messing around with the regulars at the restaurant, joking with them, remembering their little orders and making it fun. Now I mostly just keep my face straight and say what I need to say.\n\nAnd after shifts I used to text my brother, just stupid stuff sometimes, like something weird a customer said or a picture of the bus stop sign that’s been bent forever—sorry, that’s not really the point. But now I’ll see his message and just let it sit there. I don’t feel that pull to answer like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like everybody has, especially with work stuff or money being tight, but I don’t know if I’d call it the same thing or say it lasted a couple months.

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I keep feeling like I’ve really let my family down. We took on this mortgage, and I keep thinking it was my fault, like I pushed us into it or I should’ve known better. And then at work, if I mess up even something small—like an order or forgetting extra napkins—it feels like proof that I’m just useless.\n\nSorry, I know that sounds dramatic. I used to be pretty good at my job, you know, I could remember regulars’ drinks and all that. Now every little mistake just sticks in my head, and I keep thinking I deserve whatever happens because I caused all this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. It’s like this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. I’ll be at work carrying plates and smiling at people because that’s what you do, you know, the whole “how’s everything tasting?” thing, and I can hear myself saying it but I don’t really feel… anything good behind it. I’m just sort of going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after the mortgage situation happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It kind of started after we took on the condo mortgage. Not, like, the next morning exactly, but that’s when it began to settle in and just… not really leave.\n\nIt’s been about a year and eight months now, which sounds weirdly specific, but I remember because it lines up with the mortgage stuff. I keep thinking about all the paperwork and how I should’ve read things more carefully, and then I start thinking about other times I messed up money things, like even stupid stuff from years ago… but yeah, around then is when the depressed feeling really started.\n\nDoctor: Before this started around the mortgage, had you ever gone through another period in your life when you felt depressed most days for a while?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure. I mean, everybody has bad stretches, right? Like when I was younger I’d get really stressed with school or money, and I remember one semester where I basically lived on vending machine coffee and those little powdered donuts from the gas station—terrible idea, by the way, they leave that weird film in your mouth.\n\nBut depressed most days for a while like this? I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the same way. I guess this feels more… stuck than stuff before.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped enjoying things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. Like, I used to enjoy messing around with the regulars at the restaurant, joking with them, remembering their little orders and making it fun. Now I mostly just keep my face straight and say what I need to say.\n\nAnd after shifts I used to text my brother, just stupid stuff sometimes, like something weird a customer said or a picture of the bus stop sign that’s been bent forever—sorry, that’s not really the point. But now I’ll see his message and just let it sit there. I don’t feel that pull to answer like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I worry about the mortgage stuff a lot, obviously, and then sometimes it branches off into, like, whether I’m doing enough at work or if I forgot something small, like leaving the coffee pot on—though I don’t even drink much coffee at home, so that’s kind of dumb.\n\nBut “many different things,” I’m not sure. It feels more like one thought grabs another thought, if that makes sense, and then I’m just kind of stuck following it around.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Um… sleep? I don’t know, honestly. I mean, I go to bed, or I’m supposed to go to bed, and then I’ll get distracted by something stupid like whether the sheets need washing or if the neighbors are dragging bins around outside.\n\nI haven’t really kept track of it. Some nights feel worse than others, I guess, but I couldn’t tell you in a clean way. Like, it’s not like I have a chart or anything.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage situation, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most days and lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: Before the mortgage thing? I… I’m not sure. I don’t really have it sorted in my head like that, like “this was two weeks” or “that was before.” Time gets kind of mushy when I think back on it.\n\nI mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure, but whether it was most days and losing interest at the same time… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it more. I remember stuff around work more than dates, like who was on the schedule or whether the soda machine was acting up, but that doesn’t really answer you.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t sit there counting the days exactly, but most days there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m at work carrying plates around and smiling because that’s what you do, you know, people don’t want their waiter looking miserable while they’re ordering soup or whatever… but it’s not because I actually feel good.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I mean… not like I’m planning something. I don’t have, you know, a plan to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut yeah, sometimes when I’m lying there awake and I know I have to be up early for a shift, I’ll think… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could skip the whole thing and not keep messing everything up. Then I start thinking about tables I forgot to refill water for, which is stupid, but it all kind of piles up.\n\nDoctor: Has your appetite changed much since this started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, but not in one clean direction, if that makes sense. Some weeks I barely touch the staff meal, even if it’s something I usually like. I’ll just pick at it and then get busy with tables and forget it’s sitting there.\n\nThen other nights I get home and I’m suddenly eating whatever’s in the kitchen, like crackers, leftovers, cereal, just… standing there, trying to feel settled or something. It’s kind of embarrassing, because it’s not even meals exactly, it’s more like I can’t tell what I need.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage situation were suddenly resolved, do you think you would still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the mortgage thing definitely makes everything louder, like when a customer is tapping their card on the little machine before I’ve even put the bill in, and you’re already behind on three tables—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nEven if that got fixed, I think I’d still have that heavy, empty feeling most days. Like I’m carrying plates around and smiling because I’m supposed to, not because I actually feel okay. The money stress doesn’t help, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I’m tired a lot, but it’s hard to tell if it’s “unusual” tired or just, you know, restaurant tired. Being on your feet for a whole shift, carrying trays, people changing their orders after you already put them in—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really measured it. Some days I feel pretty drained, but I can’t say exactly if it’s more than before. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I notice at work. Is that the right line? Then someone asks me about ranch or a side of fries and I lose the whole thread.\n\nSo yeah, concentrating has been harder. Not all the time, but enough that it makes me second-guess myself.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you've been moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a couple people at work have said something. I don’t have, you know, a plan to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut yeah, sometimes when I’m lying there awake and I know I have to be up early for a shift, I’ll think… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could skip the whole thing and not keep messing everything up. Then I start thinking about tables I forgot to refill water for, which is stupid, but it all kind of piles up.\n\nDoctor: Has your appetite changed much since this started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, but not in one clean direction, if that makes sense. Some weeks I barely touch the staff meal, even if it’s something I usually like. I’ll just pick at it and then get busy with tables and forget it’s sitting there.\n\nThen other nights I get home and I’m suddenly eating whatever’s in the kitchen, like crackers, leftovers, cereal, just… standing there, trying to feel settled or something. It’s kind of embarrassing, because it’s not even meals exactly, it’s more like I can’t tell what I need.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage situation were suddenly resolved, do you think you would still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the mortgage thing definitely makes everything louder, like when a customer is tapping their card on the little machine before I’ve even put the bill in, and you’re already behind on three tables—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nEven if that got fixed, I think I’d still have that heavy, empty feeling most days. Like I’m carrying plates around and smiling because I’m supposed to, not because I actually feel okay. The money stress doesn’t help, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I’m tired a lot, but it’s hard to tell if it’s “unusual” tired or just, you know, restaurant tired. Being on your feet for a whole shift, carrying trays, people changing their orders after you already put them in—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really measured it. Some days I feel pretty drained, but I can’t say exactly if it’s more than before. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I notice at work. Like with the POS, I’ll be putting in table numbers or splitting checks, and I just don’t trust that I entered it right, even when it’s stuff I’ve done for years.\n\nAnd inventory sheets too—numbers that should be simple, cases of soda or whatever, I’ll stare at the screen like… wait, did I just type that in? Is that the right line? Then someone asks me about ranch or a side of fries and I lose the whole thread.\n\nSo yeah, concentrating has been harder. Not all the time, but enough that it makes me second-guess myself.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you've been moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a couple people at work have said something. Not like a big sit-down or anything, more like, “You okay?” because I’ll either be pacing around, checking the same tables or receipts over and over, like I forgot if I already did it.\n\nOr the opposite happens, where I feel like I’m moving through syrup. Like grabbing menus and walking over to the POS just takes me longer than it should. And in a restaurant that’s awkward, because everyone can kind of see when you’re not moving right.\n\nDoctor: Before this began around the mortgage, was there ever another time in your life when you felt this same kind of depression for a couple of months or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like everybody has, especially with work stuff or money being tight, but I don’t know if I’d call it the same thing or say it lasted a couple months.\n\nIt’s hard for me to separate it out, honestly. Time gets kind of blurry when you’re just doing shifts and sleeping weird hours. Like restaurant schedules make everything feel like one long week sometimes. So… maybe? But I can’t point to a clear time and say, “Yes, that was the same depression for months.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean… two weeks is harder, because that’s shorter, so maybe? But I still don’t know if I’d call it “most of the day nearly every day.”

But now I’ll see his message and just let it sit there. I don’t feel that pull to answer like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I worry about the mortgage stuff a lot, obviously, and then sometimes it branches off into, like, whether I’m doing enough at work or if I forgot something small, like leaving the coffee pot on—though I don’t even drink much coffee at home, so that’s kind of dumb.\n\nBut “many different things,” I’m not sure. It feels more like one thought grabs another thought, if that makes sense, and then I’m just kind of stuck following it around.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Um… sleep? I don’t know, honestly. I mean, I go to bed, or I’m supposed to go to bed, and then I’ll get distracted by something stupid like whether the sheets need washing or if the neighbors are dragging bins around outside.\n\nI haven’t really kept track of it. Some nights feel worse than others, I guess, but I couldn’t tell you in a clean way. Like, it’s not like I have a chart or anything.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage situation, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most days and lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: Before the mortgage thing? I… I’m not sure. I don’t really have it sorted in my head like that, like “this was two weeks” or “that was before.” Time gets kind of mushy when I think back on it.\n\nI mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure, but whether it was most days and losing interest at the same time… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it more. I remember stuff around work more than dates, like who was on the schedule or whether the soda machine was acting up, but that doesn’t really answer you.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t sit there counting the days exactly, but most days there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m at work carrying plates around and smiling because that’s what you do, you know, people don’t want their waiter looking miserable while they’re ordering soup or whatever… but it’s not because I actually feel good.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I mean… not like I’m planning something. I don’t have, you know, a plan to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut yeah, sometimes when I’m lying there awake and I know I have to be up early for a shift, I’ll think… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could skip the whole thing and not keep messing everything up. Then I start thinking about tables I forgot to refill water for, which is stupid, but it all kind of piles up.\n\nDoctor: Has your appetite changed much since this started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, but not in one clean direction, if that makes sense. Some weeks I barely touch the staff meal, even if it’s something I usually like. I’ll just pick at it and then get busy with tables and forget it’s sitting there.\n\nThen other nights I get home and I’m suddenly eating whatever’s in the kitchen, like crackers, leftovers, cereal, just… standing there, trying to feel settled or something. It’s kind of embarrassing, because it’s not even meals exactly, it’s more like I can’t tell what I need.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage situation were suddenly resolved, do you think you would still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the mortgage thing definitely makes everything louder, like when a customer is tapping their card on the little machine before I’ve even put the bill in, and you’re already behind on three tables—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nEven if that got fixed, I think I’d still have that heavy, empty feeling most days. Like I’m carrying plates around and smiling because I’m supposed to, not because I actually feel okay. The money stress doesn’t help, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I’m tired a lot, but it’s hard to tell if it’s “unusual” tired or just, you know, restaurant tired. Being on your feet for a whole shift, carrying trays, people changing their orders after you already put them in—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really measured it. Some days I feel pretty drained, but I can’t say exactly if it’s more than before. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I notice at work. Like with the POS, I’ll be putting in table numbers or splitting checks, and I just don’t trust that I entered it right, even when it’s stuff I’ve done for years.\n\nAnd inventory sheets too—numbers that should be simple, cases of soda or whatever, I’ll stare at the screen like… wait, did I just type that in? Is that the right line? Then someone asks me about ranch or a side of fries and I lose the whole thread.\n\nSo yeah, concentrating has been harder. Not all the time, but enough that it makes me second-guess myself.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you've been moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a couple people at work have said something. Not like a big sit-down or anything, more like, “You okay?” because I’ll either be pacing around, checking the same tables or receipts over and over, like I forgot if I already did it.\n\nOr the opposite happens, where I feel like I’m moving through syrup. Like grabbing menus and walking over to the POS just takes me longer than it should. And in a restaurant that’s awkward, because everyone can kind of see when you’re not moving right.\n\nDoctor: Before this began around the mortgage, was there ever another time in your life when you felt this same kind of depression for a couple of months or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like everybody has, especially with work stuff or money being tight, but I don’t know if I’d call it the same thing or say it lasted a couple months.\n\nIt’s hard for me to separate it out, honestly. Time gets kind of blurry when you’re just doing shifts and sleeping weird hours. Like restaurant schedules make everything feel like one long week sometimes. So… maybe? But I can’t point to a clear time and say, “Yes, that was the same depression for months.”\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage situation, was there ever a time when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that for sure. Like, before the mortgage thing, I had times where I was down or worn out, but “most of the day nearly every day” for two months… I don’t know.\n\nThere were stretches where work was just… a lot, and I’d come home and not want to do anything, but then restaurant schedules mess with your head anyway. You work doubles, you sleep at weird times, you eat standing up—sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nI guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t point to a clear two-month period like that before all this.\n\nDoctor: Before this started around the mortgage, did you ever have another time in your life when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I mean… two weeks is harder, because that’s shorter, so maybe? But I still don’t know if I’d call it “most of the day nearly every day.”\n\nLike I’ve definitely had bad weeks before, especially when work piled up or I was just exhausted, but then sometimes a good shift would happen or I’d get distracted by regulars coming in—there’s this one older couple who used to come every Thursday and always ask for the same booth, and for some reason that could kind of reset my mood for a bit.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t clearly remember another time before the mortgage where it was that steady for two weeks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really have a clean answer for that. I know I had bad patches before the mortgage stuff, but whether it was nearly every day for two weeks… I can’t honestly say.

Sometimes it was more like I’d have a rough run of shifts, or I’d be wiped out and everything felt heavier, but then something would break it up. Like a day off, or a decent night at work, or just getting busy enough that I wasn’t thinking about it. Restaurant time is strange like that—weeks kind of smear together.

So… maybe there were times I was down, but I can’t point to one and say yes, it was nearly every day for two weeks before the mortgage thing.

I’ll be at work carrying plates and smiling at people because that’s what you do, you know, the whole “how’s everything tasting?” thing, and I can hear myself saying it but I don’t really feel… anything good behind it. I mean, everybody has bad stretches, right? Like when I was younger I’d get really stressed with school or money, and I remember one semester where I basically lived on vending machine coffee and those little powdered donuts from the gas station—terrible idea, by the way, they leave that weird film in your mouth.\n\nBut depressed most days for a while like this? I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the same way. Like, I used to enjoy messing around with the regulars at the restaurant, joking with them, remembering their little orders and making it fun. Now I mostly just keep my face straight and say what I need to say.\n\nAnd after shifts I used to text my brother, just stupid stuff sometimes, like something weird a customer said or a picture of the bus stop sign that’s been bent forever—sorry, that’s not really the point. But now I’ll see his message and just let it sit there. I don’t feel that pull to answer like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, I worry about the mortgage stuff a lot, obviously, and then sometimes it branches off into, like, whether I’m doing enough at work or if I forgot something small, like leaving the coffee pot on—though I don’t even drink much coffee at home, so that’s kind of dumb.\n\nBut “many different things,” I’m not sure. I don’t know, honestly. I mean, I go to bed, or I’m supposed to go to bed, and then I’ll get distracted by something stupid like whether the sheets need washing or if the neighbors are dragging bins around outside.\n\nI haven’t really kept track of it. Some nights feel worse than others, I guess, but I couldn’t tell you in a clean way. Like, it’s not like I have a chart or anything.\n\nDoctor: Before the mortgage situation, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most days and lost interest in things?\n\nPatient: Before the mortgage thing? I… I’m not sure. I don’t really have it sorted in my head like that, like “this was two weeks” or “that was before.” Time gets kind of mushy when I think back on it.\n\nI mean, I’ve had low stretches, sure, but whether it was most days and losing interest at the same time… I don’t know. I’d have to think about it more. I remember stuff around work more than dates, like who was on the schedule or whether the soda machine was acting up, but that doesn’t really answer you.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t sit there counting the days exactly, but most days there’s this heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Like I’m at work carrying plates around and smiling because that’s what you do, you know, people don’t want their waiter looking miserable while they’re ordering soup or whatever… but it’s not because I actually feel good.\n\nIt’s more like I’m just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I mean… not like I’m planning something. I don’t have, you know, a plan to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nBut yeah, sometimes when I’m lying there awake and I know I have to be up early for a shift, I’ll think… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could skip the whole thing and not keep messing everything up. Then I start thinking about tables I forgot to refill water for, which is stupid, but it all kind of piles up.\n\nDoctor: Has your appetite changed much since this started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed, but not in one clean direction, if that makes sense. Some weeks I barely touch the staff meal, even if it’s something I usually like. I’ll just pick at it and then get busy with tables and forget it’s sitting there.\n\nThen other nights I get home and I’m suddenly eating whatever’s in the kitchen, like crackers, leftovers, cereal, just… standing there, trying to feel settled or something. It’s kind of embarrassing, because it’s not even meals exactly, it’s more like I can’t tell what I need.\n\nDoctor: If the mortgage situation were suddenly resolved, do you think you would still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I mean, the mortgage thing definitely makes everything louder, like when a customer is tapping their card on the little machine before I’ve even put the bill in, and you’re already behind on three tables—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nEven if that got fixed, I think I’d still have that heavy, empty feeling most days. Like I’m carrying plates around and smiling because I’m supposed to, not because I actually feel okay. The money stress doesn’t help, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I’m tired a lot, but it’s hard to tell if it’s “unusual” tired or just, you know, restaurant tired. Being on your feet for a whole shift, carrying trays, people changing their orders after you already put them in—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really measured it. Is that the right line? Then someone asks me about ranch or a side of fries and I lose the whole thread.\n\nSo yeah, concentrating has been harder. Not all the time, but enough that it makes me second-guess myself.\n\nDoctor: Have other people noticed that you've been moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a couple people at work have said something. Not like a big sit-down or anything, more like, “You okay?” because I’ll either be pacing around, checking the same tables or receipts over and over, like I forgot if I already did it.\n\nOr the opposite happens, where I feel like I’m moving through syrup. Like grabbing menus and walking over to the POS just takes me longer than it should. I don’t know if I can separate it out cleanly before the mortgage thing. I mean, the feeling I know is… most days there’s this heavy, empty thing in my chest, like I’m just going through the motions.
